Iran is not known to currently possess [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction "Weapons of mass destruction") and has signed treaties repudiating the development and possession of WMD including the [Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty%5Fon%5Fthe%5FNon-Proliferation%5Fof%5FNuclear%5FWeapons "Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons"),[\[1\]](#cite%5Fnote-npt-1) [Biological Weapons Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological%5FWeapons%5FConvention "Biological Weapons Convention"),[\[2\]](#cite%5Fnote-OPBW-2) [Chemical Weapons Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical%5FWeapons%5FConvention "Chemical Weapons Convention"),[\[3\]](#cite%5Fnote-status-3) and [Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive%5FNuclear-Test-Ban%5FTreaty "Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty"). The [nuclear program of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fprogram%5Fof%5FIran "Nuclear program of Iran") has been one of the most scrutinized in the world; Iran asserts it is purely civilian, while the IAEA Board of Governors has found Iran in non-compliance with its [International Atomic Energy Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") (IAEA) obligations. Iran has called for [nuclear-weapon states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List%5Fof%5Fstates%5Fwith%5Fnuclear%5Fweapons#Recognized%5Fnuclear-weapon%5Fstates "List of states with nuclear weapons") to [disarm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fdisarmament "Nuclear disarmament") and the establishment of a [Middle East Weapons of Mass Destruction Free Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle%5FEast%5Fnuclear%5Fweapon%5Ffree%5Fzone "Middle East nuclear weapon free zone").[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4) Over 100,000 Iranian troops and civilians were victims of [Iraqi chemical attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi%5Fchemical%5Fattacks%5Fagainst%5FIran "Iraqi chemical attacks against Iran") during the 1980s [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Iraq%5FWar "Iran–Iraq War").[\[5\]](#cite%5Fnote-Bahgat-5)[\[6\]](#cite%5Fnote-r1-6)

Development of nuclear technology began in the [Pahlavi era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi%5FIran "Pahlavi Iran") and continued after the 1979 [Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FRevolution "Iranian Revolution"). The [United States Intelligence Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5FIntelligence%5FCommunity "United States Intelligence Community") assessed in 2007 that Iran pursued nuclear weapons under the [AMAD Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMAD%5FProject "AMAD Project") between the late 1980s until 2003, then ceasing its effort.[\[7\]](#cite%5Fnote-7) In 2005, the IAEA Board of Governors found Iran in non-compliance with its NPT safeguards agreement[\[8\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEAIran2005-8)[\[9\]](#cite%5Fnote-9) and in 2006 the [United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United Nations Security Council") (UNSC) demanded Iran cease [uranium enrichment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium%5Fenrichment "Uranium enrichment")[\[10\]](#cite%5Fnote-10)[\[11\]](#cite%5Fnote-11) and [imposed sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5Fsanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "International sanctions against Iran").[\[12\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNNews2-12)[\[13\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranSanction2-13)[\[14\]](#cite%5Fnote-14)[\[15\]](#cite%5Fnote-15) Throughout the early 2010s, US,[\[16\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated4-16) EU,[\[17\]](#cite%5Fnote-17) and Russian[\[16\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated4-16) officials said Iran was pursuing [nuclear latency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Flatency "Nuclear latency"), but not weapons possession. The US and Israel sought to covertly degrade the Iranian nuclear program, with the 1990s US [Operation Merlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation%5FMerlin "Operation Merlin"), the 2009 use of the jointly-developed [Stuxnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuxnet "Stuxnet") computer worm, and [Israeli assassinations of Iranian nuclear scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassinations%5Fof%5FIranian%5Fnuclear%5Fscientists "Assassinations of Iranian nuclear scientists") since 2010, part of the [Iran–Israel proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Israel%5Fproxy%5Fconflict "Iran–Israel proxy conflict").

In 2015, the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") was signed by Iran with the [P5+1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P5+1 "P5+1"), China, France, Russia, the UK, the US, plus Germany: Iran agreed to extensive monitoring and restriction of activities at [nuclear facilities in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Ffacilities%5Fin%5FIran "Nuclear facilities in Iran") including [Arak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arak%5FNuclear%5FComplex "Arak Nuclear Complex"), [Fordow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordow%5FUranium%5FEnrichment%5FPlant "Fordow Uranium Enrichment Plant"), [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan%5FNuclear%5FTechnology/Research%5FCenter "Isfahan Nuclear Technology/Research Center"), [Natanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natanz%5FNuclear%5FFacility "Natanz Nuclear Facility"), in exchange for sanctions relief. In 2018, US president [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald%5FTrump "Donald Trump") [unilaterally withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fwithdrawal%5Ffrom%5Fthe%5FJoint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "United States withdrawal from the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") from the plan, imposing a [maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum%5Fpressure%5Fcampaign "Maximum pressure campaign") of sanctions, and Iran began stockpiling enriched uranium[\[a\]](#cite%5Fnote-HEU-18)[\[18\]](#cite%5Fnote-know-19)[\[19\]](#cite%5Fnote-Byman-20)[\[20\]](#cite%5Fnote-sweeping-21) and largely suspended IAEA monitoring.[\[18\]](#cite%5Fnote-know-19)[\[21\]](#cite%5Fnote-happens-22)

On 12 June 2025, the IAEA found[\[b\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-23) Iran [non-compliant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background%5Fto%5Fthe%5FIran–Israel%5Fwar#Iran%5Fnuclear%5Fdeal,%5FU.S.%5Fwithdrawal,%5Fand%5Fnon-compliance "Background to the Iran–Israel war") with its NPT safeguards agreement for the first time since 2005.[\[22\]](#cite%5Fnote-Murphy-24)[\[23\]](#cite%5Fnote-nuclearwatchdog-25) On 13 June, Israel launched airstrikes targeting Iranian military leaders, nuclear scientists, and nuclear facilities, beginning the twelve-day [Iran–Israel war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day%5FWar "Twelve-Day War"). On 22 June, the [United States bombed Iranian nuclear sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025%5FUnited%5FStates%5Fstrikes%5Fon%5FIranian%5Fnuclear%5Fsites "2025 United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites") with [larger bunker-buster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-57A/B%5FMOP "GBU-57A/B MOP") bombs. Iran subsequently suspended operation with the IAEA. In August, France, Germany, and the UK triggered the [snapback mechanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snapback%5Fmechanism%5Fof%5Fsanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "Snapback mechanism of sanctions against Iran"),[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-26) which reinstated UN sanctions in September. On 18 October, Iran, Russia, and China declared the JCPOA terminated and the UN sanctions legally void.[\[25\]](#cite%5Fnote-27) In February 2026, Israel and the United States launched a [war against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026%5FIran%5Fwar "2026 Iran war"), with the supposed pursuit of "weapons of mass destruction" under the Iranian nuclear and missile programs as one justification.[\[26\]](#cite%5Fnote-28)[\[27\]](#cite%5Fnote-29)

The [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps Aerospace Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%5FRevolutionary%5FGuard%5FCorps%5FAerospace%5FForce "Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps Aerospace Force")'s acquisition of missile technologies, especially [ballistic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic%5Fmissile "Ballistic missile"), has also led to sanctions. In 2010, UNSC [Resolution 1929](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil%5FResolution%5F1929 "United Nations Security Council Resolution 1929") prohibited Iran from ballistic missile technology, although it is disputed if the 2015 UNSC Resolution 2231 overturned this.[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-30) Iran supplies missiles to its [allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis%5Fof%5FResistance "Axis of Resistance"), particularly the [Houthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthis "Houthis") and [Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah "Hezbollah").[\[29\]](#cite%5Fnote-31)

### Overview

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=2%20%22Edit%20section:%20Overview%22)\]

In September 2005, the IAEA Board of Governors, in a rare non-consensus decision with 12 [abstentions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstention "Abstention"),[\[30\]](#cite%5Fnote-32) recalled a previous Iranian "policy of concealment" regarding its enrichment program[\[31\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2003/75-33) and found that Iran had violated its NPT Safeguards Agreement.[\[32\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2005/77-34) Another IAEA report stated "there is no evidence that the previously undeclared nuclear material and activities ... were related to a nuclear weapons program."[\[31\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2003/75-33) Iran has said that the military threat posed by Israel and the United States is forcing it to restrict the release of information on its nuclear program.[\[33\]](#cite%5Fnote-35) [Gawdat Bahgat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gawdat%5FBahgat "Gawdat Bahgat") of the [National Defense University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National%5FDefense%5FUniversity%5F%28Washington,%5FD.C.%29 "National Defense University (Washington, D.C.)") speculates that Iran may have a lack of confidence in the international community which was reinforced when many nations, under pressure from the United States, rejected or withdrew from signed commercial deals with the Iranian nuclear authority.[\[34\]](#cite%5Fnote-Gawdat%5FBahgat-36)

On 31 July 2006, the UN Security Council passed a resolution demanding Iran suspend its enrichment program.[\[35\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNSEC%5FRes-37) On 23 December 2006, the Security Council imposed [sanctions against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "Sanctions against Iran"),[\[12\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNNews2-12) which were tightened on 24 March 2007,[\[13\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranSanction2-13) because Iran refused to suspend enrichment. Iran's representative to the UN argued that the sanctions compelled Iran to abandon its rights under the NPT to peaceful nuclear technology.[\[12\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNNews2-12) The [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned%5FMovement "Non-Aligned Movement") called on both sides to work through the [IAEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") for a solution.[\[36\]](#cite%5Fnote-ReutersNAM-38)

US intelligence predicted in August 2005 that Iran could have the key ingredients for a nuclear weapon by 2015.[\[37\]](#cite%5Fnote-39) On 25 October 2007, the United States declared the [Revolutionary Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%5FRevolutionary%5FGuard%5FCorps "Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps") a "proliferator of weapons of mass destruction", and the [Quds Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quds%5FForce "Quds Force") a "supporter of terrorism".[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-BBC-USLabel-40) Iran responded that "it is incongruent for a country \[US\] who itself is a producer of weapons of mass destruction to take such a decision."[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-BBC-USLabel-40) [Mohamed ElBaradei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed%5FElBaradei "Mohamed ElBaradei"), Director General of the IAEA at the time, said he had no evidence Iran was building nuclear weapons and accused US leaders of adding "fuel to the fire" with their rhetoric.[\[39\]](#cite%5Fnote-41) Speaking in Washington in November 2007, days before the IAEA was to publish its latest report, Israeli deputy prime minister [Shaul Mofaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaul%5FMofaz "Shaul Mofaz") called for ElBaradei to be sacked, saying: "The policies followed by ElBaradei endanger world peace. His irresponsible attitude of sticking his head in the sand over Iran's nuclear programme should lead to his impeachment." Israel and some western governments fear Iran is using its nuclear programme as a covert means to develop weapons, while Iran says it is aimed solely at producing electricity. For its part in the conflict-ridden Middle East, Israel is a member of the IAEA, but it is not itself a signatory to the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty, and is widely believed to currently be the only nuclear-armed state in the region.[\[40\]](#cite%5Fnote-42)

### History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=3%20%22Edit%20section:%20History%22)\]

Iran's nuclear program began as a result of the Cold War alliance between the United States and the Shah of Iran, [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FReza%5FPahlavi "Mohammad Reza Pahlavi"), who emerged as an important US ally in the Persian Gulf.[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-iiss.org-43) Under the [Atoms for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoms%5Ffor%5FPeace "Atoms for Peace") program, Iran received basic nuclear research facilities from the United States. In return, Tehran signed the [Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty%5Fon%5Fthe%5FNon-Proliferation%5Fof%5FNuclear%5FWeapons "Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons") (NPT) in 1968\. Fueled by high oil prices in the 1970s, Iran sought to purchase large-scale nuclear facilities from Western suppliers in order to develop nuclear power and fuel-cycle facilities with both civilian and potential military applications.[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-iiss.org-43) In March 1974, the shah established the [Atomic Energy Organization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic%5FEnergy%5FOrganization%5Fof%5FIran "Atomic Energy Organization of Iran") (AEOI).[\[42\]](#cite%5Fnote-44) Sensing a heightened risk of nuclear proliferation, the United States convinced Western allies to limit the export of nuclear fuel-cycle facilities to Iran. Supreme Leader Ayatollah [Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah%5FKhomeini "Ruhollah Khomeini"), whose revolution displaced the Shah's monarchy in 1979 and ruled the newly established [Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%5FRepublic%5Fof%5FIran "Islamic Republic of Iran") until his death in 1989, placed little emphasis on nuclear weapons development because it was viewed as a suspicious Western innovation.[\[43\]](#cite%5Fnote-45) During that time, many of Iran's top scientists fled the country while the United States organized an international campaign to block any nuclear assistance to Iran.

Following the death of Ayotollah Khomeini, the leadership of President [Ali Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FAkbar%5FHashemi%5FRafsanjani "Ali Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani") and Supreme Leader [Ali Hosseini Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FHosseini%5FKhamenei "Ali Hosseini Khamenei") sought to revive Iran's overt nuclear civilian program and expand undeclared nuclear activities during the 1990s.[\[44\]](#cite%5Fnote-46) According to a strategic dossier from [International Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FInstitute%5Ffor%5FStrategic%5FStudies "International Institute for Strategic Studies"), Iran turned away from Western suppliers and obtained nuclear assistance from Russia and China in a number of key areas, including uranium mining, milling and conversion, as well as technology for heavy-water research reactors.[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-iiss.org-43) However, Washington intervened with Moscow and Beijing to prevent Iran from fully acquiring its list of nuclear power and fuel-cycle facilities. The 1990s also saw Iran expand its furtive nuclear research into conversion, enrichment and plutonium separation. "Most importantly, on the basis of additional centrifuge assistance from the [A.Q. Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.Q.%5FKhan "A.Q. Khan") network, Iran was able to begin the construction of pilot-scale and industrial-scale enrichment facilities at Natanz around 2000."[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-iiss.org-43) Full exposure of Iran's nuclear activities came in 2002, when an Iranian exiled opposition group, the [National Council of Resistance of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National%5FCouncil%5Fof%5FResistance%5Fof%5FIran "National Council of Resistance of Iran") (NCRI) declared the Natanz project in August of that year. Since that time, international pressure on Iran has remained steady, hampering but not halting the country's nuclear development.[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-iiss.org-43) Iran remains legally bound to the NPT and states its support for the treaty.

On 28 August 2025, E3 members, France, Germany, and the United Kingdom, initiated the process of the snapback mechanism, with the prospect of freezing Iranian overseas assets, blocking arms deals with Iran, imposing penal action against development of Iran's ballistic missile program and further restricting Iran's military and nuclear activities.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-26) In a letter addressed to the president of the UN Security Council, the foreign ministers of the E3 stated that since 2019, Iran had "increasingly and deliberately ceased performing its JCPOA commitments", including "the accumulation of a highly enriched uranium stockpile which lacks any credible civiliian justification and is unprecedented for a state without a nuclear weapons program".[\[45\]](#cite%5Fnote-:53-47) The letter detailed additional Iranian violations of the agreement despite the fact that the E3 "have consistently upheld their agreements under the terms of the JCPOA".[\[45\]](#cite%5Fnote-:53-47) The activation opened a 30-day window, intended ro reengage Iran, "whose refusal to cooperate with the International Atomic Energy Agency's (IAEA) inspectors started the crisis",[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-26) in diplomatic negotiations before full restoration of sanctions.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-26) According to Euronews, Iran's Foreign Minister [Abbas Araghchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas%5FAraghchi "Abbas Araghchi") declared that it was "unjustified, illegal, and lacking any legal basis" and promised that "The Islamic Republic of Iran will respond appropriately".[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-26)

There are various estimates of when Iran might be able to produce a nuclear weapon, should it choose to do so:

* A 2005 assessment by the [International Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FInstitute%5Ffor%5FStrategic%5FStudies "International Institute for Strategic Studies") concluded "if Iran threw caution to the wind, and sought a nuclear weapon capability as quickly as possible without regard for international reaction, it might be able to produce enough HEU for a single nuclear weapon by the end of this decade", assuming no technical problems. The report concludes, however, that it is unlikely that Iran would flatly ignore international reactions and develop nuclear weapons anyway.[\[46\]](#cite%5Fnote-IISSassessment-48)
* A 2005 US [National Intelligence Estimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National%5FIntelligence%5FEstimate "National Intelligence Estimate") stated that Iran was ten years from making a nuclear weapon.[\[47\]](#cite%5Fnote-49)
* In 2006 [Ernst Uhrlau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst%5FUhrlau "Ernst Uhrlau"), the head of German [intelligence service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundesnachrichtendienst "Bundesnachrichtendienst"), said Tehran would not be able to produce enough material for a nuclear bomb before 2010 and would only be able to make it into a weapon by about 2015.[\[48\]](#cite%5Fnote-50)
* A 2007 annual review the International Institute for Strategic Studies in London stated that "If and when Iran does have 3,000 centrifuges operating smoothly, the IISS estimates it would take an additional 9-11 months to produce 25 kg of highly enriched uranium, enough for one implosion-type weapon. That day is still 2–3 years away at the earliest."[\[49\]](#cite%5Fnote-IISS2007assessment-51)
* The former head of the IAEA, Mohamed ElBaradei, said on 24 May 2007 that Iran could take between 3 and 8 years to make a bomb if it went down that route.[\[49\]](#cite%5Fnote-IISS2007assessment-51)
* On 22 October 2007, Mohamed ElBaradei repeated that, even assuming Iran was trying to develop a nuclear bomb, they would require "between another three and eight years to succeed", an assessment shared by "all the intelligence services".[\[50\]](#cite%5Fnote-irishtimes-20071022-52)
* In December 2007, the United States [National Intelligence Estimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National%5FIntelligence%5FEstimate "National Intelligence Estimate") (representing the consensus view of all 16 American intelligence agencies) concluded with a "high level of confidence" that Iran had halted its nuclear weapons program in 2003 and "with moderate confidence" that the program remained frozen as of mid-2007\. The new estimate says that the enrichment program could still provide Iran with enough raw material to produce a nuclear weapon sometime by the middle of next decade, but that intelligence agencies "_do not know whether it currently intends to develop nuclear weapons_" at some future date.[\[51\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated3-53)[\[52\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated2-54) Iranian Foreign Minister [Manouchehr Mottaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manouchehr%5FMottaki "Manouchehr Mottaki") said 70 percent of the U.S. report was "true and positive," but denied its allegations of Iran having had a nuclear weapons program before 2003\. Russia has said there was no proof Iran has ever run a nuclear weapons program.[\[53\]](#cite%5Fnote-55) The former head of the IAEA, Mohamed ElBaradei, stated that he had seen "maybe some studies about possible weaponization", but "no evidence" of "an active weaponization program" as of October 2007.[\[54\]](#cite%5Fnote-IHT%5F102907-56) [Thomas Fingar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas%5FFingar "Thomas Fingar"), former chairman of the National Intelligence Council until December 2008, in reference to the 2007 Iran NIE and using intelligence to anticipate opportunities and shape the future, said intelligence has a "recently reinforced propensity to underscore, overstate, or 'hype' the findings in order to get people to pay attention" and that the 2007 NIE was intended to send the message "you do not have a lot of time but you appear to have a diplomatic or non-military option".[\[55\]](#cite%5Fnote-57) A National Intelligence Estimate (NIE) is the most authoritative written judgment concerning a national security issue prepared by the Director of Central Intelligence.[\[56\]](#cite%5Fnote-58)
* The U.S. Director of National Intelligence said in February 2009 that Iran would not realistically be able to a get a nuclear weapon until 2013, if it chose to develop one.,[\[57\]](#cite%5Fnote-ReutEst-59) and that US intelligence does not know whether Iran intends to develop nuclear weapons, but believes Iran could at least be keeping the option to develop them open.[\[58\]](#cite%5Fnote-60) Mossad Chief [Meir Dagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meir%5FDagan "Meir Dagan") was more cautious, saying recently that it would take the Iranians until 2014\. German, French, and British intelligence said that under a worst-case scenario it would take Iran a minimum of 18 months to develop a nuclear weapon if it chose to build one, and it would have to first purify its uranium and weaponize its uranium.[\[57\]](#cite%5Fnote-ReutEst-59) An anonymous source in the German Foreign Intelligence Service (BND) whose rank was not provided has gone further and claimed Iran could produce a nuclear bomb and conduct an underground test in 6 months if it wanted to and further asserted that Iran had already mastered the full uranium enrichment cycle, and possessed enough centrifuges to produce weapons-grade uranium.[\[59\]](#cite%5Fnote-stern-61) Physicists said that if Iran were to choose to develop a nuclear weapon, it would have to withdraw from the International Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty and expel International Atomic Energy Agency inspectors from the country.[\[60\]](#cite%5Fnote-62) [George Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George%5FFriedman "George Friedman"), head of the global intelligence company [Stratfor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratfor "Stratfor"), has said Iran is "decades away" from developing any credible nuclear-arms capacity.[\[61\]](#cite%5Fnote-online.barrons.com-63)
* On 12 February 2010 US think tank expert [David Albright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David%5FAlbright "David Albright"), the head of the Institute for Science and International Security, said in a report that Iran was seeking to "make sufficient weapons-grade uranium".
* An IAEA report issued 8 November 2011 provided detailed information outlining the IAEA's concerns about the possible military dimensions of Iran's nuclear program, noting that Iran had pursued a structured program or activities relevant to the development of nuclear weapons.[\[62\]](#cite%5Fnote-64)
* On 30 April 2018, Israeli prime minister [Binyamin Netanyahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binyamin%5FNetanyahu "Binyamin Netanyahu") revealed thousands of files he said were copied from a "highly secret location" in Teheran which show an Iranian effort to develop nuclear weapons between 1999 and 2003.[\[63\]](#cite%5Fnote-65)
* On 1 May 2018 the IAEA reiterated its 2015 report, saying it had found no credible evidence of nuclear weapons activity in Iran after 2009.[\[64\]](#cite%5Fnote-:0-66)[\[65\]](#cite%5Fnote-:1-67)[\[66\]](#cite%5Fnote-:2-68)
* In 2021, a group of former Republican officials including ex-[CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA "CIA") director [James Woolsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James%5FWoolsey "James Woolsey"), wrote in the [National Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National%5FReview "National Review") that "Iran probably has already the atomic bomb," citing past detection failures by U.S. intelligence and the IAEA.[\[67\]](#cite%5Fnote-69)\[_[dubious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy%5Fdispute#Disputed%5Fstatement "Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute") – [discuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction#Dubious "Talk:Iran and weapons of mass destruction")_\]
* On 2 March 2026, IAEA Director General [Rafael Grossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael%5FGrossi "Rafael Grossi") reported that while Iran maintained a significant stockpile of near-weapons-grade uranium, there was no evidence of a structured program to build a nuclear bomb.[\[68\]](#cite%5Fnote-70)

### IAEA

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=4%20%22Edit%20section:%20IAEA%22)\]

The [International Atomic Energy Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") (IAEA) is an autonomous international organization that seeks to promote the peaceful use of nuclear energy and to inhibit its use for military purposes.

On 6 March 2006, the IAEA Secretariat reported that "_the Agency has not seen indications of diversion of nuclear material to nuclear weapons or other nuclear explosive devices ... however, after three years of intensive verification, there remain uncertainties with regard to both the scope and the nature of Iran's nuclear programme_".[\[69\]](#cite%5Fnote-iaea%5Fstatement-71) However, the inspectors did find some sensitive documents, including instructions and diagrams on how to make [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") into a [sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere "Sphere"), which is only necessary to make nuclear weapons. Iran furnished the IAEA with copies, claiming not to have used the information for weapons work, which it had obtained along with other technology and parts in 1987 and the mid-1990s.\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] It is thought this material was sold to them by [Abdul Qadeer Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul%5FQadeer%5FKhan "Abdul Qadeer Khan"),\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] though the documents did not have the necessary technical details to actually manufacture a bomb.

On 18 December 2003, Iran voluntarily signed, but did not ratify or bring into force, an Additional Protocol that allows IAEA inspectors access to individuals, documentation relating to procurement, [dual-use equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-use%5Ftechnology "Dual-use technology"), certain military-owned workshops, and research and development locations.[\[70\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2005/87-72) Iran agreed voluntarily to implement the Additional Protocol provisionally, however when the IAEA reported Iran's non-compliance to the [United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United Nations Security Council") on 4 February 2006 Iran withdrew from its voluntary adherence to the Additional Protocol.[\[71\]](#cite%5Fnote-HCFAC-20080220-73)

On 12 May 2006, claims that highly enriched uranium (well over the 3.5% enriched level) was reported to have been found "at a site where Iran has denied such sensitive atomic work", appeared. "They have found particles of highly enriched uranium \[HEU\], but it is not clear if this is contamination from centrifuges that had been previously found \[from imported material\] or something new," said one diplomat close to the UN International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA). These reports have not yet been officially confirmed by the IAEA (as of 1 June 2006).[\[72\]](#cite%5Fnote-Highly%5FEnriched%5FDetected%5F1-74)[\[73\]](#cite%5Fnote-Highly%5FEnriched%5FDetected%5F2-75)[\[74\]](#cite%5Fnote-Highly%5FEnriched%5FDetected%5F3-76)

On 31 July 2006, the [United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United Nations Security Council") passed a resolution demanding that Iran suspend its uranium enrichment activities.[\[35\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNSEC%5FRes-37)

In late 2006, "New traces of plutonium and enriched uranium– potential material for atomic warheads– have been found \[by the IAEA\] in a nuclear waste facility in Iran." However, "A senior U.N. official who was familiar with the report cautioned against reading too much into the findings of traces of highly enriched uranium and plutonium, saying Iran had explained both and they could plausibly be classified as byproducts of peaceful nuclear activities."[\[75\]](#cite%5Fnote-77) In 2007 these traces were determined to have come from leaking used highly enriched uranium fuel from the [Tehran Research Reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran%5FResearch%5FReactor "Tehran Research Reactor"), which the U.S. supplied to Iran in 1967, and the matter was closed.[\[76\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2007/48-78)

In July 2007 the IAEA announced that Iran has agreed to allow inspectors to visit its Arak nuclear plant, and by August 2007 a plan for monitoring the Natanz uranium enrichment plant will have been finalised.[\[77\]](#cite%5Fnote-79)

In August 2007 the IAEA announced that Iran has agreed to a plan to resolve key questions regarding its past nuclear activities. The IAEA described this as a "significant step forward".[\[78\]](#cite%5Fnote-80)

In September 2007 the IAEA announced it has been able to verify that Iran's declared nuclear material has not been diverted from peaceful use. While the IAEA has been unable to verify some "important aspects" regarding the nature and scope of Iran's nuclear work, the agency and Iranian officials agreed on a plan to resolve all outstanding issues, Director-General Mohamed ElBaradei said at the time.[\[79\]](#cite%5Fnote-81) In an interview with [Radio Audizioni Italiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio%5FAudizioni%5FItaliane "Radio Audizioni Italiane") the same month, ElBaradei remarked that "Iran does not constitute a certain and immediate threat for the international community".[\[80\]](#cite%5Fnote-82) In October 2007, ElBaradei amplified these remarks, telling [Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le%5FMonde "Le Monde") that, even if Iran did intend to develop a nuclear bomb, they would need "between another three and eight years to succeed". He went on to note that "all the intelligence services" agree with this assessment and that he wanted to "get people away from the idea that Iran will be a threat from tomorrow, and that we are faced right now with the issue of whether Iran should be bombed or allowed to have the bomb".[\[50\]](#cite%5Fnote-irishtimes-20071022-52)

In late October 2007, according to the [International Herald Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FHerald%5FTribune "International Herald Tribune"), the former head of the IAEA, Mohamed ElBaradei, stated that he had seen "no evidence" of Iran developing nuclear weapons. The IHT quoted ElBaredei as stating that,

> "We have information that there has been maybe some studies about possible weaponization," said Mohamed ElBaradei, who led the International Atomic Energy Agency. "That's why we have said that we cannot give Iran a pass right now, because there is still a lot of question marks."
> 
> "But have we seen Iran having the nuclear material that can readily be used into a weapon? No. Have we seen an active weaponization program? No."

The IHT report went on to say that "ElBaradei said he was worried about the growing rhetoric from the U.S., which he noted focused on Iran's alleged intentions to build a nuclear weapon rather than evidence the country was actively doing so. If there is actual evidence, ElBaradei said he would welcome seeing it."[\[54\]](#cite%5Fnote-IHT%5F102907-56)

In November 2007 ElBaradei circulated a report to the upcoming meeting of the IAEA Board of Governors.[\[81\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2007/58-83)[\[82\]](#cite%5Fnote-84)[\[83\]](#cite%5Fnote-85) Its findings conclude that Iran has made important strides towards clarifying its past activities, including provided access to documentation and officials involved in centrifuge design in the 1980s and 1990s. Answers provided by Iran regarding the past P-1 and P-2 centrifuge programs were found to be consistent with the IAEA's own findings. However, Iran has ignored the demands of the UN Security council, and has continued to enrich uranium in the past year. The IAEA is not able to conclusively confirm that Iran isn't currently enriching uranium for military purposes, as its inspections have been restricted to workshops previously declared as part of the civilian uranium enrichment program, and requests for access to certain military workshops have been denied; the report noted that "As a result, the agency's knowledge about Iran's current nuclear program is diminishing". The report also confirmed that Iran now possesses 3000 centrifuges, a 10-fold increase over the past year, though the feed rate is below the maximum for a facility of this design. Data regarding the P-2 centrifuge, which Ahmadinejad has claimed will quadruple production of enriched uranium, was provided only several days before the report was published; the IAEA plan to discuss this issue further in December. In response to the report the US has vowed to push for more sanctions, whilst Iran has called for an apology from the US.[\[84\]](#cite%5Fnote-86)

In his final November 2009 statement to the IAEA Board of Governors, Mohamed ElBaradei said the agency continued to verify the non-diversion of declared nuclear material in Iran, but that other issues of concern had reached a "dead end" unless Iran were to fully cooperate with the agency. ElBaradei stated it would be helpful if "we were able to share with Iran more of the material that is at the centre of these concerns", and also said it would be helpful if Iran fully implemented the Subsidiary Arrangements to its Safeguards Agreement and fully implemented the Additional Protocol. ElBaradei said Iran's failure to report the existence of a new fuel enrichment facility until September 2009 was inconsistent with its obligations under the Subsidiary Arrangements to its Safeguards Agreement. ElBaradei closed by saying international negotiations represented a "unique opportunity to address a humanitarian need and create space for negotiations".[\[85\]](#cite%5Fnote-87)

On 18 February 2010 the IAEA released a new report on Iran's nuclear program. Ivan Oelrich and Ivanka Barzashka, writing in the _[Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulletin%5Fof%5Fthe%5FAtomic%5FScientists "Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists")_, suggested "the media has seriously misrepresented the actual contents of the report" and that "in fact, no new information has been revealed." They wrote that there was "no independent assessment that Iran is engaged in weapons work" and that this was "hardly the first time that the agency has discussed potential evidence of Tehran's nuclear weapons research".[\[86\]](#cite%5Fnote-88) Iran's envoy to the UN atomic watchdog criticized Western powers for interpreting the IAEA report in an "exaggerated, selective and inaccurate" manner.\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In an April 2010 interview with the BBC, former IAEA Director General ElBaradei said Western nations were seeking harsher sanctions "out of frustration". "I don't think Iran is developing, or we have new information that Iran is developing, a nuclear weapon today .. there is a concern about Iran's future intentions, but even if you talk to MI6 or the CIA, they will tell you they are still four or five years away from a weapon. So, we have time to engage," he said. ElBaradei further said the building of trust between the parties would "not happen until the two sides sit around the negotiating table and address their grievances. Sooner or later that will happen."[\[87\]](#cite%5Fnote-89)

In February 2019, the IAEA certified that Iran was still abiding by the international [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") (JCPOA) of 2015.[\[88\]](#cite%5Fnote-ReutersFeb1922-90) However, on 8 May 2019, one year after U.S. withdrawal from JCPOA, Iran announced it would suspend implementation of some parts of the JCPOA, threatening further action in 60 days unless it received protection from US sanctions.[\[89\]](#cite%5Fnote-91) In July 2019, the IAEA confirmed that Iran had breached both the 300 kg enriched uranium stockpile limit and the 3.67% refinement limit.[\[90\]](#cite%5Fnote-92) On 5 November 2019, Iranian nuclear chief [Ali Akbar Salehi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FAkbar%5FSalehi "Ali Akbar Salehi") announced that Iran would enrich uranium to 5% at the [Fordow Fuel Enrichment Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordow%5FFuel%5FEnrichment%5FPlant "Fordow Fuel Enrichment Plant"), adding that the country had the capability to enrich uranium to 20% if needed.[\[91\]](#cite%5Fnote-93) Also in November, [Behrouz Kamalvandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behrouz%5FKamalvandi "Behrouz Kamalvandi"), spokesman for the Atomic Energy Organization of Iran, stated that Iran can enrich up to 60% if needed.[\[92\]](#cite%5Fnote-94) President [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan%5FRouhani "Hassan Rouhani") declared that Iran's nuclear program would be "limitless" while the country launches the third phase of quitting from the 2015 nuclear deal.[\[93\]](#cite%5Fnote-95)

In March 2020, the IAEA said that Iran had nearly tripled its stockpile of enriched uranium since early November 2019.[\[94\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA%5FMarch%5F202022-96) In September 2020, the IAEA reported that Iran had accumulated ten times as much enriched uranium as permitted by the JCPOA.[\[95\]](#cite%5Fnote-97)

Throughout 2021 and 2022, Iran installed cascades of advanced centrifuges (IR-2m, IR-4, IR-6) at Natanz and Fordow, significantly increasing its enrichment output.[\[96\]](#cite%5Fnote-jp16032122-98)[\[97\]](#cite%5Fnote-:722-99) In February 2021, the IAEA reported that Iran stopped allowing access to data from nuclear sites, as well as plans for future sites.[\[98\]](#cite%5Fnote-100) In April 2021, a sabotage attack struck the Natanz enrichment plant, causing an electrical blackout and damaging centrifuges. Iran responded by further increasing enrichment: days later, it began producing 60% enriched uranium, an unprecedented level for Iran, just short of weapons-grade (90% and above). This 60% enrichment took place at Natanz, and later at Fordow as well, yielding a stockpile that as of early 2023 exceeded \~70 kg of 60% uranium.[\[97\]](#cite%5Fnote-:722-99) If Iran chose to enrich this material to 90%, it would be sufficient for several nuclear warheads. The UK, France, and Germany said that Iran has "no credible civilian use for uranium metal" and called the news "deeply concerning" because of its "potentially grave military implications".[\[99\]](#cite%5Fnote-101) On 25 June 2022, in a meeting with the senior diplomat of the EU, [Ali Shamkhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FShamkhani "Ali Shamkhani"), Iran's top security officer, declared that Iran would continue to advance its nuclear program until the West modifies its "illegal behavior".[\[100\]](#cite%5Fnote-102)

In July 2022, according to an IAEA report referred to by Reuters, Iran had increased its uranium enrichment through the use of sophisticated equipment at its underground Fordow plant in a configuration that can more quickly vary between enrichment levels.[\[101\]](#cite%5Fnote-103) In September 2022, Germany, United Kingdom and France expressed doubts over Iran's sincerity in returning to the JCPOA after Tehran insisted that the IAEA close its probes into uranium traces at three undeclared Iranian sites.[\[102\]](#cite%5Fnote-104) The IAEA said it could not guarantee the peaceful nature of Iran's nuclear program, stating there had been "no progress in resolving questions about the past presence of nuclear material at undeclared sites".[\[103\]](#cite%5Fnote-105) United Nations Secretary-General [António Guterres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/António%5FGuterres "António Guterres") urged Iran to hold "serious dialogue" about nuclear inspections and said IAEA's independence is "essential" in response to Iranian demands to end probes.[\[104\]](#cite%5Fnote-106) In February 2023, the IAEA reported having found uranium in Iran enriched to 84%.[\[105\]](#cite%5Fnote-107) The Iranian government has claimed that this is an "unintended fluctuation" in the enrichment levels, though the Iranians have been openly enriching uranium to 60% purity, a breach of the 2015 nuclear deal.[\[106\]](#cite%5Fnote-108) In 2024, Iranian President [Masoud Pezeshkian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masoud%5FPezeshkian "Masoud Pezeshkian") expressed interest in reopening discussions with the United States on the nuclear deal.[\[107\]](#cite%5Fnote-109)[\[108\]](#cite%5Fnote-110)

In November 2024, Iran announced that it would make new advanced centrifuges after IAEA condemned Iranians' non-compliance and secrecy.[\[109\]](#cite%5Fnote-111)[\[110\]](#cite%5Fnote-112)

On 31 May 2025, IAEA reported that Iran had sharply increased its stockpile of uranium enriched to 60% purity, just below weapons-grade, reaching over 408 kilograms, a nearly 50% rise since February.[\[111\]](#cite%5Fnote-apnews2025061222-113) The agency warned that this amount is enough for multiple nuclear weapons if further enriched. It also noted that Iran remains the only non-nuclear-weapon state to produce such material, calling the situation a "serious concern".[\[111\]](#cite%5Fnote-apnews2025061222-113) The [IAEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") reported that Iran was not meeting its obligations per its 2019 agreement by hiding its development of nuclear material, and the IAEA voted to censure Iran on June 12.[\[112\]](#cite%5Fnote-114)[\[113\]](#cite%5Fnote-115) That same month, the NCRI said Iran is pursuing nuclear weapons through a new program called the "Kavir Plan". According to the NCRI, the new project involves six sites in [Semnan province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semnan%5Fprovince "Semnan province") working on warheads and related technology, succeeding the previous AMAD Project.[\[114\]](#cite%5Fnote-116)[\[115\]](#cite%5Fnote-117)

In February 2026, Iran informed the IAEA that normal safeguards were "legally untenable and materially impracticable," as a result of threats and "acts of aggression," leaving the IAEA unable to verify whether Iran had suspended enrichment or confirm the status of its current stockpile,[\[116\]](#cite%5Fnote-AP%5FNews%5FIAEA%5FPost%5FWar-118) though it found no evidence Iran was weaponizing.[\[117\]](#cite%5Fnote-119)

#### Alleged weaponization studies

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=5%20%22Edit%20section:%20Alleged%20weaponization%20studies%22)\]

See also: [Nuclear program of Iran § February 2008 report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fprogram%5Fof%5FIran#February%5F2008%5Freport "Nuclear program of Iran")

Former IAEA Director General ElBaradei said in 2009 that the agency had been provided with "no credible evidence" that Iran is developing nuclear weapons,[\[118\]](#cite%5Fnote-GuaNoEv-120) but the _New York Times_ reported in January 2009 that the IAEA is investigating U.S. allegations _Project 110_ and _Project 111_ could be names for Iranian efforts for designing a nuclear warhead and making it work with an Iranian missile.[\[119\]](#cite%5Fnote-nyt20080111-121) "We are looking to those suppliers of information to help us on the question of authenticity, because that is really a major issue. It is not an issue that involves nuclear material; it's a question of allegations," ElBaradei further said.[\[120\]](#cite%5Fnote-AEOI%5FPC-122) ElBaradei has strongly denied reports that the agency had concluded Iran had developed technology needed to assemble a nuclear warhead,[\[121\]](#cite%5Fnote-123) when a November 2009 article in [The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The%5FGuardian "The Guardian") said the allegations included Iran's weapon design activities using [two-point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fweapon%5Fdesign#Fusion-boosted%5Ffission%5Fweapons "Nuclear weapon design") implosion designs.[\[122\]](#cite%5Fnote-124)

The _New York Times_ article cited classified US intelligence reports asserting that Professor [Mohsen Fakhrizadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohsen%5FFakhrizadeh "Mohsen Fakhrizadeh") is in charge of the projects, while Iranian officials assert these projects are a fiction made up by the United States.[\[119\]](#cite%5Fnote-nyt20080111-121) The article further reported that "while the international agency readily concedes that the evidence about the two projects remains murky, one of the documents it briefly displayed at a meeting of the agency's member countries in Vienna last year, from Mr. Fakrizadeh's projects, showed the chronology of a missile launching, ending with a warhead exploding about 650 yards above ground – approximately the altitude from which the bomb dropped on Hiroshima was detonated."[\[119\]](#cite%5Fnote-nyt20080111-121) Gordon Oehler, who ran the CIA's nonproliferation center and served as deputy director of the presidential commission on weapons of mass destruction, wrote "if someone has a good idea for a missile program, and he has really good connections, he'll get that program through.. But that doesn't mean there is a master plan for a nuclear weapon."[\[123\]](#cite%5Fnote-ACW%5FID-125) Outside experts note that the parts of the report made public lack many dates associated with Iran's alleged activities meaning it is possible Iran had a Project 110 at one time, but scrapped it as US intelligence insists.[\[124\]](#cite%5Fnote-126) The _[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington%5FPost "Washington Post")_ reported that "nowhere are there construction orders, payment invoices, or more than a handful of names and locations possibly connected to the projects."[\[125\]](#cite%5Fnote-127) Former IAEA Director General ElBaradei said the agency didn't have any information that nuclear material has been used and didn't have any information that any components of nuclear weapons had been manufactured.[\[120\]](#cite%5Fnote-AEOI%5FPC-122) Iran has asserted that the documents are a fabrication, while the IAEA has urged Iran to be more cooperative and Member States to provide more information about the allegations to be shared with Iran.[\[126\]](#cite%5Fnote-128)

In August 2009 an article in the Israeli newspaper [Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz "Haaretz") alleged that ElBaradei had "censored" evidence obtained by IAEA inspectors over the preceding few months.[\[127\]](#cite%5Fnote-129) ElBaradei has angrily rejected claims from Israel, France and the US that he had suppressed the internal IAEA report, saying all relevant and confirmed information had been presented to member states.[\[118\]](#cite%5Fnote-GuaNoEv-120) ElBaradei said he and the agency have repeatedly said the rumors of censorship were "totally baseless, totally groundless. All information that we have received that has been vetted, assessed in accordance with our standard practices, has been shared with the Board."[\[120\]](#cite%5Fnote-AEOI%5FPC-122)

On 16 November 2009 the Director General provided a report to the Board of Governors. The report stated "there remain a number of outstanding issues which give rise to concerns, and which need to be clarified to exclude the existence of possible military dimensions to Iran's nuclear programme." "The Agency is still awaiting a reply from Iran to its request to meet relevant Iranian authorities in connection with these issues", the report said. The report further said, "it would be helpful if Member States which have provided documentation to the agency would agree to share more of that documentation with Iran, as appropriate."[\[128\]](#cite%5Fnote-130)[\[129\]](#cite%5Fnote-131)

Russia has denied allegations of "continued Russian assistance to Iran's nuclear weapons program" as "totally groundless" and said the November 2009 IAEA report reaffirmed the absence of a military component in Iran's efforts in the nuclear field.[\[130\]](#cite%5Fnote-132)

In December 2009, _[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The%5FTimes "The Times")_ claimed that a document from an unnamed Asian intelligence agency described the use of a neutron source which has no use other than in a nuclear weapon, and claimed the document appeared to be from an office in Iran's Defense Ministry and may have been from around 2007.[\[131\]](#cite%5Fnote-133)[\[132\]](#cite%5Fnote-134) Norman Dombey, professor emeritus of theoretical physics at [Sussex University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sussex%5FUniversity "Sussex University"), wrote in that "nothing in the published 'intelligence documents' shows Iran is close to having nuclear weapons" and argued that it is "unlikely that nuclear weapon projects would be distributed among several universities, or weapon parts marketed to research centres."[\[133\]](#cite%5Fnote-135) A senior U.N. official who saw the document said it may or may not be authentic, that it was unclear when the document was written, and that it was unclear whether any experiments had ever actually been performed.[\[134\]](#cite%5Fnote-136) The C.I.A. did not declare whether it believes the document was real, and European spy agencies also did not give any authentication to the document.[\[135\]](#cite%5Fnote-NYTPuzzled-137) Western intelligence agencies said that, if genuine, it was unclear whether the paper provided any new insights into the state of Iranian weapons research.[\[135\]](#cite%5Fnote-NYTPuzzled-137) "It's very troubling – if real," said Thomas B. Cochran, a senior scientist in the nuclear program of the [Natural Resources Defense Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural%5FResources%5FDefense%5FCouncil "Natural Resources Defense Council").[\[135\]](#cite%5Fnote-NYTPuzzled-137) The Institute for Science and International Security, said that it "urges caution and further assessment" of the document and noted that "the document does not mention nuclear weapons .. and we have seen no evidence of an Iranian decision to build them."[\[135\]](#cite%5Fnote-NYTPuzzled-137) Anton Khlopkov, the founding director of the Center for Energy and Security Studies, said the media leak may be being used "as a pretext for inciting the campaign against Iran."[\[136\]](#cite%5Fnote-138) Former Russian prime minister [Yevgeny Primakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny%5FPrimakov "Yevgeny Primakov") has also said after the public publications of the documents "Russia has no concrete information that Iran is planning to construct a weapon".[\[137\]](#cite%5Fnote-Russ09-139) Russia's representative to the IAEA, Alexander Zmeyevskiy, has noted that though the IAEA is in possession of these documents, the IAEA's findings "do not contain any conclusions about the presence of undeclared nuclear activities in Iran."[\[138\]](#cite%5Fnote-140) Iran pointed out the claims had not been verified by the International Atomic Energy Agency and argued that "some countries are angry that our people defend their nuclear rights."[\[139\]](#cite%5Fnote-141) "I think that some of the claims about our nuclear issue have turned into a repetitive and tasteless joke," Iranian president Mahmoud Ahmadinejad said in response to the documents.[\[140\]](#cite%5Fnote-142)

In early July 2025, Iran suspended co-operation with the IAEA.[\[141\]](#cite%5Fnote-abc-no-iaea-2jul25-143) The last IAEA inspectors left Iran by 4 July.[\[142\]](#cite%5Fnote-reuter-insp-out-5jul25-144)

### Iranian stance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=6%20%22Edit%20section:%20Iranian%20stance%22)\]

Iran states that the purpose of its nuclear program is the generation of power and that any other use would be a violation of the [Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5FNon-Proliferation%5FTreaty "Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty"), of which it is a signatory, as well as being against Islamic religious principles. Iran claims that nuclear power is necessary for a booming population and rapidly industrialising nation. It points to the fact that Iran's population has more than doubled in 20 years, the country regularly imports gasoline and electricity, and that burning fossil fuel in large amounts harms Iran's environment drastically. Additionally, Iran questions why it shouldn't be allowed to diversify its sources of energy, especially when there are fears of its oil fields eventually being depleted. It continues to argue that its valuable oil should be used for high value products and export, not simple electricity generation. Furthermore, Iran argues that nuclear power makes fairly good economic sense. Building reactors is expensive, but subsequent operating costs are low and stable, and increasingly competitive as fossil-fuel prices rise.[\[143\]](#cite%5Fnote-145) Iran also raises funding questions, claiming that developing the excess capacity in its oil industry would cost it $40 billion, not to speak of paying for the power plants. Harnessing nuclear power costs a fraction of this, considering Iran has abundant supplies of accessible [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") ore.[\[144\]](#cite%5Fnote-146) These claims have been echoed by [Scott Ritter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott%5FRitter "Scott Ritter"), the former UN weapons inspector in Iraq.[\[145\]](#cite%5Fnote-147) Roger Stern, of Johns Hopkins Department of Geography and Environmental Engineering, agrees "Iran's claims to need nuclear power could be genuine".[\[146\]](#cite%5Fnote-148)

Iran states it has a legal right to enrich uranium for peaceful purposes under the [NPT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5FNon-Proliferation%5FTreaty "Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty"), and further says that it "has constantly complied with its obligations under the NPT and the Statute of the International Atomic Energy Agency".[\[147\]](#cite%5Fnote-InfCirc724-149) Twelve other countries are known to operate [uranium enrichment facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enriched%5Furanium#Global%5Fenrichment%5Ffacilities "Enriched uranium"). Iran states that "the failure of certain Nuclear- Weapon States to fulfill their international obligations continue to be a source of threat for the international community".[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4) Iran also states that "the only country that has ever used nuclear weapons still maintains a sizable arsenal of thousands of nuclear warheads" and calls for a stop to the transfer of technology to non-NPT states.[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4) Iran has called for the development of a follow-up committee to ensure compliance with global nuclear disarmanent.[\[148\]](#cite%5Fnote-150) Iran and many other nations without nuclear weapons have said that the present situation whereby Nuclear Weapon States monopolise the right to possess nuclear weapons is "highly discriminatory", and they have pushed for steps to accelerate the process of nuclear disarmament.[\[149\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated1998-151)

Iran has criticized the European Union because it believes it has taken no steps to reduce the danger of nuclear weapons in the Middle East.[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4) Iran has called on the state of Israel to sign the NPT, accept inspection of its nuclear facilities, and place its nuclear facilities under IAEA safeguards.[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4) Iran has proposed that the Middle East be established as a proposed Nuclear Weapon Free Zone.[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranDAConf-4)

On 3 December 2004, Iran's former president and an Islamic cleric,[Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar%5FHashemi%5FRafsanjani "Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani") alluded to Iran's position on nuclear energy:

> God willing, we expect to soon join the club of the countries that have a nuclear industry, with all its branches, except the military one, in which we are not interested. We want to get what we're entitled to. I say unequivocally that for no price will we be willing to relinquish our legal and international right. I also say unequivocally to those who make false claims: Iran is not pursuing nuclear weapons, but it will not give up its rights. Your provocation will not make us pursue nuclear weapons. We hope that you come to your senses soon and do not get the world involved in disputes and crises.[\[150\]](#cite%5Fnote-152)

On 14 November 2004, Iran's chief nuclear negotiator said that his country agreed to voluntarily and temporarily suspend the uranium enrichment program after pressure from the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European%5FUnion "European Union") on behalf of the United Kingdom, France and Germany, as a confidence-building measure for a reasonable period of time, with six months mentioned as a reference.

Iranian president [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad") has publicly stated Iran is not developing nuclear weapons. On 9 August 2005 Iran's Supreme Leader, [Ayatollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah "Ayatollah") [Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FKhamenei "Ali Khamenei"), issued a [fatwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatwa "Fatwa") that the production, stockpiling and use of nuclear weapons are forbidden under Islam and that Iran shall never acquire these weapons. The text of the fatwa has not been released although it was referenced in an official statement at a meeting of the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) in Vienna.[\[151\]](#cite%5Fnote-fatwa-153)

[Iranian President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President%5Fof%5FIran "President of Iran") [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad") in a 2005 speech to the [U.N. General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FGeneral%5FAssembly "United Nations General Assembly") said "We are concerned that once certain powerful states completely control nuclear energy resources and technology, they will deny access to other states and thus deepen the divide between powerful countries and the rest of the international community ... peaceful use of nuclear energy without possession of a nuclear fuel cycle is an empty proposition".[\[152\]](#cite%5Fnote-154)

On 6 August 2005, Iran rejected a 34-page European Union proposal intended to help Iran build "a safe, economically viable and proliferation-proof civil nuclear power generation and research program." The Europeans, with US agreement, intended to entice Iran into a binding commitment not to develop uranium enrichment capability by offering to provide fuel and other long-term support that would facilitate electricity generation with nuclear energy. Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman Hamid Reza Asefi rejected the proposal saying, "We had already announced that any plan has to recognize Iran's right to enrich uranium".[\[153\]](#cite%5Fnote-aljazeera9356-155) After the Iranian Revolution, Germany halted construction of the Bushehr reactor, the United States cut off supply of highly enriched uranium (HEU) fuel for the Tehran Research Reactor, and Iran never received uranium from France which it asserted it was entitled to. Russia agreed not to provide an enrichment plant and terminated cooperation on several other nuclear-related technologies, including laser isotope separation; China terminated several nuclear projects (in return, in part for entry into force of a U.S.-China civil nuclear cooperation agreement); and Ukraine agreed not to provide the turbine for Bushehr. Iran argues that these experiences contribute to a perception that foreign nuclear supplies are potentially subject to being interrupted.[\[154\]](#cite%5Fnote-156)

Iran resumed its [uranium enrichment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enriched%5Furanium "Enriched uranium") program in January 2006, prompting the IAEA to refer the issue to the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United Nations Security Council").

On 21 February 2006, [Rooz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rooz "Rooz"), a news website run by Iranian exiles (the Fedayeen Khalq \[People's Commandos\] leftist terrorist group),[\[155\]](#cite%5Fnote-157) reported that Hojatoleslam Mohsen Gharavian, a student of Qom's fundamentalist cleric [Mesbah Yazdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FTaghi%5FMesbah%5FYazdi "Mohammad Taghi Mesbah Yazdi"), spoke about the necessity of using nuclear weapons as a means to retaliate and announced that "based on religious law, everything depends on our purpose".[\[156\]](#cite%5Fnote-rooz21feb06-158) In an interview with the [Islamic Republic News Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%5FRepublic%5FNews%5FAgency "Islamic Republic News Agency") the same day, Gharavian rejected these reports, saying "We do not seek nuclear weapons and the Islamic religion encourages coexistence along with peace and friendship...these websites have tried to misquote me."[\[157\]](#cite%5Fnote-IRNA21feb06-159)

On 11 April 2006, Iranian president [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad") announced Iranian scientists working at the pilot facility at [Natanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natanz "Natanz") had successfully enriched uranium to the 3.5 percent level, using a small cascade of 164 [gas centrifuges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas%5Fcentrifuge "Gas centrifuge"). In the televised address from the city of [Mashhad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashhad "Mashhad") he said, "I am officially announcing that Iran has joined the group of those countries which have [nuclear technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Ftechnology "Nuclear technology")".[\[158\]](#cite%5Fnote-npr5336802-160)

In May 2006 some members of the Iranian legislature ("[Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis "Majlis")" or Parliament) sent a letter to UN Secretary-General [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi%5FAnnan "Kofi Annan") threatening to withdraw from the NPT if Iran's right to peaceful use of nuclear technology under the treaty was not protected.[\[159\]](#cite%5Fnote-161)

On 21 February 2007, the same day the UN deadline to suspend nuclear activities expired, [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad") made the following statement: "If they say that we should close down our fuel production facilities to resume talks, we say fine, but those who enter talks with us should also close down their nuclear fuel production activities". The [White House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White%5FHouse "White House")'s spokesperson [Tony Snow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony%5FSnow "Tony Snow") rejected the offer and called it a "false offer".[\[160\]](#cite%5Fnote-162)

Iran has said that U.N. Security Council sanctions aimed at curtailing its uranium-enrichment activities unfairly target its medical sector. "We have thousands of patients a month at our hospital alone .. If we can't help them, some will die. It's as simple as that," said an Iranian nuclear medicine specialist. An Iranian Jew from California claimed "I don't believe in these sanctions... They hurt normal people, not leaders. What is the use of that?" Vice President of the Atomic Energy Organization of Iran Ghannadi framed the debate as a humanitarian issue, "This is about human beings. . . . When someone is sick, we should give medicine." Iran informed the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) that fuel obtained from Argentina in 1993 would run out by the end of 2010, and that it could produce the uranium itself or buy the uranium from abroad.[\[161\]](#cite%5Fnote-163)

In February 2010, to refuel the [Tehran Research Reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Ffacilities%5Fin%5FIran "Nuclear facilities in Iran") which produces [medical isotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical%5Fisotopes "Medical isotopes"),[\[162\]](#cite%5Fnote-164) Iran began using a single cascade to enrich uranium "up to 19.8%",[\[163\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA2010-10-165)[\[164\]](#cite%5Fnote-166) to match the previously foreign supplied fuel.[\[165\]](#cite%5Fnote-INFCIRC/97/Add.2-167) 20% is the upper threshold for [low enriched uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low%5Fenriched%5Furanium "Low enriched uranium") (LEU).[\[166\]](#cite%5Fnote-168) Though HEU enriched to levels exceeding 20% is considered technically usable in a nuclear explosive device,[\[167\]](#cite%5Fnote-169) this route is much less desirable because far more material is required to achieve a sustained [nuclear chain reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fchain%5Freaction "Nuclear chain reaction").[\[168\]](#cite%5Fnote-nti.org-170) HEU enriched to 90% and above is most typically used in a weapons development program.[\[169\]](#cite%5Fnote-CFR%5FINP-171)[\[170\]](#cite%5Fnote-AIP%5FGCWNP-172)

In an interview in October 2011, President Ahmadinejad of Iran said: 

> "We have already expressed our views about nuclear bombs. We said those who are seeking to build nuclear bombs or those who stockpile, they are politically and mentally retarded. We think they are stupid because the era of nuclear bombs is over. \[Why\] for example, should Iran continue its efforts and tolerate all international treasures only to build a nuclear bomb, or a few nuclear bombs that are useless? They can never be used!"[\[171\]](#cite%5Fnote-173)

On 22 February 2012, in a meeting in Tehran with the director and officials of the Atomic Energy Organization of Iran (AEOI) and nuclear scientists, Leader of the Islamic Revolution Ayatollah Seyyed [Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FKhamenei "Ali Khamenei") said: 

> "The Iranian nation has never pursued and will never pursue nuclear weapons. There is no doubt that the decision makers in the countries opposing us know well that Iran is not after nuclear weapons because the Islamic Republic, logically, religiously and theoretically, considers the possession of nuclear weapons a grave sin and believes the proliferation of such weapons is senseless, destructive and dangerous."\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### U.S. stance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction&action=edit&section=7%20%22Edit%20section:%20U.S.%20stance%22)\]

|  | This section's **[factual accuracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy%5Fdispute "Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute") may be compromised due to out-of-date information**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. _(December 2009)_ |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

See also: [United States and weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction "United States and weapons of mass destruction")

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Secretary%5FBlinken%5FMeets%5Fwith%5FIsraeli%5FAlternate%5FPrime%5FMinister%5Fand%5FDefense%5FMinister%5FGantz%5F%2851222835918%29.jpg)

[Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe%5FBiden "Joe Biden")'s Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony%5FBlinken "Antony Blinken") did not rule out a military intervention to stop Iran from obtaining [nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List%5Fof%5Fstates%5Fwith%5Fnuclear%5Fweapons "List of states with nuclear weapons").[\[172\]](#cite%5Fnote-Blinken-174)

* In 2005, the United States stated that Iran has violated both Article III and Article II of the NPT.[\[173\]](#cite%5Fnote-175) The IAEA Board of Governors, in a rare divided vote, found Iran in noncompliance with its NPT safeguards agreement for a 1985–2003 "policy of concealment"[\[31\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEA-GOV/2003/75-33) regarding its efforts to develop enrichment and reprocessing technologies.[\[8\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEAIran2005-8) The United States,[\[174\]](#cite%5Fnote-176) the IAEA[\[175\]](#cite%5Fnote-177) and others[\[176\]](#cite%5Fnote-178) consider these technologies to be of particular concern because they can be used to produce fissile material for use in nuclear weapons.
* The United States has argued that Iran's concealment of efforts to develop sensitive nuclear technology is _prima facie_ evidence of Iran's intention to develop nuclear weapons, or at a minimum to develop a latent nuclear weapons capability. Others have noted that while possession of the technology "contributes to the latency of non-nuclear weapon states in their potential to acquire nuclear weapons" but that such latency is not necessarily evidence of intent to proceed toward the acquisition of nuclear weapons, since "intent is in the eye of the beholder".[\[177\]](#cite%5Fnote-179)
* The United States has also provided information to the IAEA on Iranian studies related to weapons design, activities, including the intention of diverting a civilian nuclear energy program to the manufacture of weapons, based on a laptop computer reportedly linked to Iranian weapons programs. The United States has pointed to other information reported by the IAEA, including the [Green Salt Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green%5FSalt%5FProject "Green Salt Project"), the possession of a document on manufacturing uranium metal hemispheres, and other links between Iran's military and its nuclear program, as further indications of a military intent to Iran's nuclear program.[\[178\]](#cite%5Fnote-180) The IAEA has said U.S. intelligence provided to it through 2007 has proven inaccurate or not led to significant discoveries inside Iran;[\[179\]](#cite%5Fnote-181) however, the US, and others have recently provided more intelligence to the agency.[\[180\]](#cite%5Fnote-autogenerated1-182)
* In May 2003, The Swiss ambassador to Iran sent the State Department a two-page document, reportedly approved by Ayatollah Khamanei, outlining a road map towards normalization of relations between the two states. The Iranians offered full transparency of its nuclear programme and withdrawal of support from [Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas "Hamas") and [Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah "Hezbollah") in exchange for security assurances and normalization of diplomatic relations. The Bush Administration did not respond to the proposal, as senior U.S. officials doubted its authenticity.[\[181\]](#cite%5Fnote-183)[\[182\]](#cite%5Fnote-184)
* The United States acknowledges Iran's right to nuclear power, and has joined with the EU-3, Russia and China in offering nuclear and other economic and technological cooperation with Iran if it suspends uranium enrichment. This cooperation would include an assured supply of fuel for Iran's nuclear reactors.[\[183\]](#cite%5Fnote-185)
* A potential reason behind U.S. resistance to an Iranian nuclear program lies in Middle Eastern geopolitics. In essence, the US feels that it must guard against even the possibility of Iran obtaining a nuclear weapons capability. Some nuclear technology is [dual-use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-use%5Ftechnology "Dual-use technology"); i.e. it can be used for peaceful energy generation, and to develop nuclear weapons, a situation that resulted in India's [nuclear weapons program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%5Fand%5Fweapons%5Fof%5Fmass%5Fdestruction "India and weapons of mass destruction") in the 1960s. A nuclear-armed Iran would dramatically change the balance of power in the Middle East, weakening US influence. It could also encourage other Middle Eastern nations to develop nuclear weapons of their own further reducing US influence in a critical region.\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]
* In 2003, the United States insisted that [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran") be "held accountable" for seeking to build nuclear arms in violation of its agreements.\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] In June 2005, the US secretary of state [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza%5FRice "Condoleezza Rice") required former [IAEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") head [Mohamed ElBaradei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed%5FElBaradei "Mohamed ElBaradei") to either "toughen his stance on Iran" or fail to be chosen for a third term as IAEA head.[\[184\]](#cite%5Fnote-186) The IAEA has on some occasions criticised the stance of the U.S. on Iran's program.[\[185\]](#cite%5Fnote-187) The United States denounced Iran's successful enrichment of uranium to fuel grade in April 2006, with spokesman [Scott McClellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott%5FMcClellan "Scott McClellan") saying, they "continue to show that Iran is moving in the wrong direction". In November 2006, Seymour Hersh described a classified draft assessment by the Central Intelligence Agency "challenging the White House's assumptions about how close Iran might be to building a nuclear bomb." He continued, "The CIA found no conclusive evidence, as yet, of a secret Iranian nuclear-weapons program running parallel to the civilian operations that Iran has declared to the International Atomic Energy Agency," adding that a current senior intelligence official confirmed the assessment.[\[186\]](#cite%5Fnote-188) On 25 February 2007, _[The Daily Telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The%5FDaily%5FTelegraph "The Daily Telegraph")_ reported that the [United States Fifth Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5FFifth%5FFleet "United States Fifth Fleet"), including the [Nimitz-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimitz%5Fclass%5Faircraft%5Fcarrier "Nimitz class aircraft carrier") [supercarriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercarrier "Supercarrier") [Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS%5FDwight%5FD.%5FEisenhower%5F%28CVN-69%29 "USS Dwight D. Eisenhower (CVN-69)"), [Nimitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS%5FNimitz%5F%28CVN-68%29 "USS Nimitz (CVN-68)") and [Stennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS%5FJohn%5FC.%5FStennis%5F%28CVN-74%29 "USS John C. Stennis (CVN-74)") "prepares to take on [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran")".[\[187\]](#cite%5Fnote-189)
* In March 2006, it was reported that the [US State Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%5FState%5FDepartment "US State Department") had opened an [Office of Iranian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office%5Fof%5FIranian%5FAffairs?action=edit&redlink=1 "Office of Iranian Affairs (page does not exist)") (OIA) – overseen by [Elizabeth Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth%5FCheney "Elizabeth Cheney"), the daughter of Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick%5FCheney "Dick Cheney"). The office's mission was reportedly to promote a democratic transition in Iran.[\[188\]](#cite%5Fnote-190) and to help "defeat" the Iranian regime.[\[189\]](#cite%5Fnote-191) Iran argued the office was tasked with drawing up plans to overthrow its government. One Iranian reformer said after the office opened that many "partners are simply too afraid to work with us anymore", and that the office had "a chilling effect".[\[190\]](#cite%5Fnote-192) The [US Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%5FCongress "US Congress") has reportedly appropriated more than $120 million to fund the project.[\[191\]](#cite%5Fnote-193)
* The Bush Administration repeatedly refused to rule out use of nuclear weapons against Iran.
